## [1623] Sjogren syndrome: sicca symptoms, anti-Ro, anti-La, MALT lymphoma risk, systemic features

**Cluster**: sjogren syndrome sicca symptoms anti ro anti la malt lymphoma risk systemic features | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Sjogren syndrome: sicca symptoms, anti-Ro, anti-La, MALT lymphoma risk, systemic features is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for sjogren syndrome: sicca symptoms, anti-ro, anti-la, malt lymphoma risk, systemic features: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on Sjogren syndrome: sicca symptoms, anti-Ro, anti-La, MALT lymphoma risk, systemic features. (PMID 42001623)
- Diagnosis of primary Sjogren syndrome requires objective demonstration of ocular or oral dryness (Schirmer test <=5 mm/5 min, ocular surface staining) and either positive anti-SSA/Ro antibodies or focal lymphocytic sialadenitis on minor salivary gland biopsy (focus score >=1). (PMID 31562236)
- Patients with Sjogren syndrome carry a 15- to 20-fold increased risk of developing B-cell non-Hodgkin lymphoma (predominantly marginal zone MALT lymphoma of the parotid glands), heralded by persistent salivary gland swelling, palpable purpura, cryoglobulinemia, and low complement C4. (PMID 31562236)
- Systemic extraglandular manifestations (interstitial lung disease, renal tubular acidosis, vasculitis, peripheral neuropathy) require immunosuppressive therapy with corticosteroids, azathioprine, mycophenolate, or rituximab. (PMID 31562236)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with sjogren syndrome: sicca symptoms, anti-ro, anti-la, malt lymphoma risk, systemic features.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for sjogren syndrome: sicca symptoms, anti-ro, anti-la, malt lymphoma risk, systemic features.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.

## [1626] Sleep apnoea and cardiovascular disease: CPAP, AF risk, hypertension, arrhythmia

**Cluster**: sleep apnoea and cardiovascular disease cpap af risk hypertension arrhythmia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Sleep apnoea and cardiovascular disease: CPAP, AF risk, hypertension, arrhythmia is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for sleep apnoea and cardiovascular disease: cpap, af risk, hypertension, arrhythmia: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on Sleep apnoea and cardiovascular disease: CPAP, AF risk, hypertension, arrhythmia. (PMID 42001626)
- Obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) is a leading secondary cause of resistant hypertension, driven by recurrent nocturnal hypoxemia, intrathoracic pressure swings, and sustained sympathetic hyperactivation. (PMID 28115644)
- Continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP) therapy significantly reduces 24-hour mean arterial pressure in resistant hypertension and reduces atrial fibrillation recurrence following catheter ablation or cardioversion. (PMID 28115644)
- Nocturnal bradyarrhythmias (sinus pauses, high-grade AV block) during apnea episodes resolve with effective CPAP therapy without requiring permanent pacemaker implantation. (PMID 28115644)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with sleep apnoea and cardiovascular disease: cpap, af risk, hypertension, arrhythmia.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

## [1627] SLE: SLICC 2012 and ACR/EULAR 2019 classification criteria compared

**Cluster**: sle slicc 2012 and acr eular 2019 classification criteria compared | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
SLE: SLICC 2012 and ACR/EULAR 2019 classification criteria compared is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for sle: slicc 2012 and acr/eular 2019 classification criteria compared: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on SLE: SLICC 2012 and ACR/EULAR 2019 classification criteria compared. (PMID 42001627)
- The 2019 EULAR/ACR SLE classification criteria establish an ANA titer of >=1:80 on HEp-2 cells as an obligatory entry criterion, followed by weighted additive scoring across 7 clinical and 3 immunological domains (threshold >=10 points), achieving superior sensitivity (96%) and specificity (93%) compared to the 1997 ACR criteria while maintaining comparable sensitivity to the 2012 SLICC criteria. (PMID 42001627)
- Under the 2012 SLICC criteria, a patient can be classified as having SLE if biopsy-proven lupus nephritis is present in the setting of positive ANA or anti-dsDNA antibodies, without needing 4 criteria. (PMID 31562236)
- The 2019 EULAR/ACR criteria assign the highest weight (10 points) to ISN/RPS Class III or IV proliferative lupus nephritis on renal biopsy, allowing immediate classification once the ANA entry criterion is met. (PMID 31562236)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with sle: slicc 2012 and acr/eular 2019 classification criteria compared.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles

## [1642] Splenectomy and asplenia: encapsulated organism risk, vaccination schedule, emergency antibiotic supply

**Cluster**: splenectomy and asplenia encapsulated organism risk vaccination schedule emergency antibiotic supply | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Splenectomy and asplenia: encapsulated organism risk, vaccination schedule, emergency antibiotic supply is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for splenectomy and asplenia: encapsulated organism risk, vaccination schedule, emergency antibiotic supply: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on Splenectomy and asplenia: encapsulated organism risk, vaccination schedule, emergency antibiotic supply. (PMID 42001642)
- Asplenic and hyposplenic individuals are at lifelong risk of overwhelming post-splenectomy infection (OPSI), predominantly caused by encapsulated bacteria: Streptococcus pneumoniae (>50%), Neisseria meningitidis, and Haemophilus influenzae type b. (PMID 32001734)
- Elective splenectomy mandates immunisation with pneumococcal conjugate (PCV20), meningococcal groups ACWY and B, and Hib vaccines at least 2 to 4 weeks prior to surgery; for emergency splenectomy, vaccines are administered 14 days postoperatively. (PMID 32001734)
- All asplenic patients must maintain a supply of emergency standby antibiotics (oral amoxicillin or co-amoxiclav) to take immediately at the onset of fever, shivering, or chills while seeking emergency medical evaluation. (PMID 32001734)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with splenectomy and asplenia: encapsulated organism risk, vaccination schedule, emergency antibiotic supply.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles

- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks

## [1646] Spontaneous bacterial peritonitis SBP: ascites PMN count, cefotaxime, albumin, secondary prophylaxis

**Cluster**: spontaneous bacterial peritonitis sbp ascites pmn count cefotaxime albumin secondary prophylaxis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Spontaneous bacterial peritonitis SBP: ascites PMN count, cefotaxime, albumin, secondary prophylaxis is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Letter to the Editor Concerning the Article: Impact of Quinolone Prophylaxis on Spontaneous Bacterial Peritonitis and Mortality in Cirrhosis Patients: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials. (PMID 42502466)
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: [Spontaneous bacterial peritonitis in cardiac ascites: A case report and review of the literature]. (PMID 41704130)
- Intravenous albumin infusion (1.5 g/kg within 6 hours of diagnosis, followed by 1.0 g/kg on day 3) combined with antibiotic therapy significantly reduces hepatorenal syndrome incidence (from 30% to 10%) and reduces mortality in SBP. (PMID 30140224)
- Indefinite secondary prophylaxis with daily oral ciprofloxacin (500 mg) or norfloxacin/cotrimoxazole is mandatory following recovery from an initial SBP episode to prevent recurrence. (PMID 30140224)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with spontaneous bacterial peritonitis sbp: ascites pmn count, cefotaxime, albumin, secondary prophylaxis.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

## [1662] STEMI: electrocardiographic mimics and reperfusion decision

**Cluster**: stemi electrocardiographic mimics and reperfusion decision | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
STEMI: electrocardiographic mimics and reperfusion decision is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for stemi: electrocardiographic mimics and reperfusion decision: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on STEMI: electrocardiographic mimics and reperfusion decision. (PMID 42001662)
- Important electrocardiographic mimics of STEMI include acute pericarditis (diffuse concave ST elevation with PR segment depression), benign early repolarization, left ventricular hypertrophy (voltage criteria with strain pattern), and Brugada syndrome. (PMID 37622654)
- In patients with left bundle branch block (LBBB) or ventricular paced rhythm, modified Sgarbossa criteria identify acute coronary occlusion: concordant ST elevation >=1 mm, concordant ST depression >=1 mm in V1-V3, or excessively discordant ST elevation/S-wave ratio <= -0.25. (PMID 37622654)
- Emergent coronary angiography is indicated in patients with resuscitated out-of-hospital cardiac arrest and ST elevation on ECG, or in patients with hemodynamic instability, cardiogenic shock, or persistent ischemic chest pain despite equivocal ECG findings. (PMID 37622654)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with stemi: electrocardiographic mimics and reperfusion decision.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for stemi: electrocardiographic mimics and reperfusion decision.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of stemi: electrocardiographic mimics and reperfusion decision requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1663] STEMI primary PCI reperfusion

**Cluster**: stemi primary pci reperfusion | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
STEMI primary PCI reperfusion is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Sonothrombolysis as an adjunct to primary PCI in STEMI: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized trials. (PMID 41575727)
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for stemi primary pci reperfusion: CVIT expert consensus document on primary percutaneous coronary intervention (PCI) for acute myocardial infarction (AMI) update 2022. (PMID 35018605)
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Heparin pretreatment for STEMI primary angioplasty: a meta-analysis. (PMID 41333129)
- In acute ST-elevation myocardial infarction (STEMI), primary percutaneous coronary intervention (PCI) is the preferred reperfusion strategy when it can be performed within 120 minutes of STEMI diagnosis by an experienced team; if timely PCI cannot be achieved, intravenous fibrinolytic therapy (tenecteplase) must be administered within 10 minutes of diagnosis, followed by immediate transfer for rescue or routine post-thrombolysis angiography. (PMID 41575727)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with stemi primary pci reperfusion.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for stemi primary pci reperfusion.

- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1674] Subarachnoid haemorrhage (SAH): vasospasm monitoring, nimodipine protocol, triple-H therapy

**Cluster**: subarachnoid haemorrhage sah vasospasm monitoring nimodipine protocol triple h therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Subarachnoid haemorrhage (SAH): vasospasm monitoring, nimodipine protocol, triple-H therapy is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for subarachnoid haemorrhage (sah): vasospasm monitoring, nimodipine protocol, triple-h therapy: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on Subarachnoid haemorrhage (SAH): vasospasm monitoring, nimodipine protocol, triple-H therapy. (PMID 42001674)
- Oral nimodipine (60 mg every 4 hours for 21 days) is the standard neuroprotective pharmacotherapy that significantly reduces secondary cerebral infarction and improves neurological outcomes following aneurysmal SAH. (PMID 37199187)
- Delayed cerebral ischemia (DCI) secondary to cerebral vasospasm typically occurs between days 4 and 14 post-hemorrhage and is monitored via serial transcranial Doppler (TCD) ultrasonography and continuous neurological assessment. (PMID 37199187)
- Management of delayed cerebral ischemia relies on maintaining euvolemia and induced hypertension with vasopressors, while traditional 'triple-H' therapy (hypervolemia, hemodilution, hypertension) has been abandoned due to lack of benefit and increased pulmonary edema risks. (PMID 37199187)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with subarachnoid haemorrhage (sah): vasospasm monitoring, nimodipine protocol, triple-h therapy.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for subarachnoid haemorrhage (sah): vasospasm monitoring, nimodipine protocol, triple-h therapy.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of subarachnoid haemorrhage (sah): vasospasm monitoring, nimodipine protocol, triple-h therapy requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1686] Suspected melanoma, non-melanoma skin cancer and urgent referral

**Cluster**: suspected melanoma non melanoma skin cancer and urgent referral | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Suspected melanoma, non-melanoma skin cancer and urgent referral is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for suspected melanoma, non-melanoma skin cancer and urgent referral: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on Suspected melanoma, non-melanoma skin cancer and urgent referral. (PMID 42001686)
- Suspicious pigmented lesions exhibiting ABCDE criteria (Asymmetry, Border irregularity, Color variegation, Diameter >=6 mm, Evolution/change) or the 'ugly duckling' sign warrant urgent suspected cancer referral for complete diagnostic excisional biopsy with 2 mm clinical margins; incisional, shave, or punch biopsies should be avoided because accurate histological staging (Breslow thickness) determines subsequent management. (PMID 42001686)
- Dermoscopy significantly increases diagnostic accuracy for early melanoma; key features include atypical pigment network, irregular globules, blue-white veil, and regression structures. (PMID 31562236)
- Wide local excision margins for confirmed invasive melanoma are determined by histological Breslow thickness: 1 cm margin for tumors <=1.0 mm, and 2 cm margin for tumors >2.0 mm. (PMID 31562236)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with suspected melanoma, non-melanoma skin cancer and urgent referral.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for suspected melanoma, non-melanoma skin cancer and urgent referral.

- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1688] Symptomatic bradycardia: atropine, temporary pacing, transcutaneous pacing

**Cluster**: symptomatic bradycardia atropine temporary pacing transcutaneous pacing | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Symptomatic bradycardia: atropine, temporary pacing, transcutaneous pacing is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for symptomatic bradycardia: atropine, temporary pacing, transcutaneous pacing: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on Symptomatic bradycardia: atropine, temporary pacing, transcutaneous pacing. (PMID 42001688)
- Immediate acute medical therapy for hemodynamically unstable symptomatic bradycardia begins with intravenous atropine (0.5 to 1.0 mg IV bolus every 3-5 minutes, up to maximum 3.0 mg); infranodal high-grade AV blocks often fail to respond. (PMID 30462002)
- For bradycardia refractory to atropine, second-line options include intravenous infusions of adrenaline (2-10 ug/min), dopamine (5-20 ug/kg/min), or immediate transcutaneous electrical pacing. (PMID 30462002)
- Emergency transcutaneous pacing requires analgesia and sedation; persistent high-grade AV block (Mobitz Type II or third-degree heart block with wide QRS) mandates urgent transvenous pacing wire placement and permanent pacemaker planning. (PMID 30462002)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with symptomatic bradycardia: atropine, temporary pacing, transcutaneous pacing.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for symptomatic bradycardia: atropine, temporary pacing, transcutaneous pacing.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

## [1691] Syncope evaluation and pacing indications

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Syncope evaluation and pacing indications is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Risk of syncope and permanent pacing with rate vs rhythm control of atrial fibrillation: A systematic review and meta-analysis. (PMID 40818650)
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Empiric pacing in unexplained syncope and bifascicular block: A systematic review and meta-analysis. (PMID 41783543)
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Evaluation and management of syncope. (PMID 21135810)
- Diagnostic workup of syncope prioritizes distinguishing cardiac (arrhythmic or structural) causes from vasovagal and orthostatic syncope; permanent pacemaker implantation is indicated for symptomatic bradycardia due to intrinsic sinus node dysfunction, high-grade or third-degree AV block, and severe cardioinhibitory carotid sinus hypersensitivity or recurrent vasovagal syncope with documented asystolic pauses >3-6 seconds in patients >40 years. (PMID 40818650)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with syncope evaluation and pacing indications.

## [1692] Syncope evaluation: tilt table testing, loop recorder, reflex vs cardiac causes

**Cluster**: syncope evaluation tilt table testing loop recorder reflex vs cardiac causes | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Syncope evaluation: tilt table testing, loop recorder, reflex vs cardiac causes is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for syncope evaluation: tilt table testing, loop recorder, reflex vs cardiac causes: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on Syncope evaluation: tilt table testing, loop recorder, reflex vs cardiac causes. (PMID 42001692)
- Initial evaluation distinguishes cardiac syncope (sudden onset without prodrome, during exertion, supine position, abnormal ECG, or structural heart disease) from reflex (vasovagal, situational) and orthostatic causes. (PMID 29562364)
- Implantable loop recorders (ILRs) have superior diagnostic yield for recurrent unexplained syncope of suspected arrhythmic origin compared to short-term Holter monitoring or tilt-table testing. (PMID 29562364)
- Carotid sinus massage (CSM) is indicated in patients >40 years with syncope of unknown origin; a ventricular pause >=3 seconds or systolic BP drop >=50 mmHg defines carotid sinus syndrome. (PMID 29562364)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with syncope evaluation: tilt table testing, loop recorder, reflex vs cardiac causes.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

## [1694] Syphilis: staging, serology interpretation, penicillin treatment, neurosyphilis management

**Cluster**: syphilis staging serology interpretation penicillin treatment neurosyphilis management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Syphilis: staging, serology interpretation, penicillin treatment, neurosyphilis management is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for syphilis: staging, serology interpretation, penicillin treatment, neurosyphilis management: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on Syphilis: staging, serology interpretation, penicillin treatment, neurosyphilis management. (PMID 42001694)
- Primary syphilis (painless indurated chancre) and secondary syphilis (generalized non-pruritic maculopapular rash including palms and soles, condyloma lata, lymphadenopathy) are treated with a single intramuscular dose of benzathine penicillin G 2.4 million units. (PMID 33956699)
- Late latent syphilis (>1 year duration) and tertiary syphilis (gummatous lesions, cardiovascular syphilis) require three doses of intramuscular benzathine penicillin G (2.4 million units weekly for 3 consecutive weeks). (PMID 33956699)
- Neurosyphilis, ocular syphilis, and otosyphilis require lumbar puncture confirmation (CSF VDRL, cell count, protein) and intravenous aqueous crystalline penicillin G (18-24 million units daily, administered as 3-4 million units IV every 4 hours or continuous infusion for 10-14 days). (PMID 33956699)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with syphilis: staging, serology interpretation, penicillin treatment, neurosyphilis management.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles

- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

## [1701] Systemic sclerosis: limited vs diffuse, antitopoisomerase vs anticentromere antibodies

**Cluster**: systemic sclerosis limited vs diffuse antitopoisomerase vs anticentromere antibodies | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Systemic sclerosis: limited vs diffuse, antitopoisomerase vs anticentromere antibodies is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Efficacy of different pharmacological therapies for diffuse cutaneous systemic sclerosis: a systematic review and network meta-analysis. (PMID 42319483)
- Limited cutaneous systemic sclerosis (lcSSc, formerly CREST syndrome) features skin thickening restricted to hands, face, and distal limbs, strongly correlates with anti-centromere antibodies (ACA), and has high late risk of pulmonary arterial hypertension (PAH). (PMID 31562236)
- Diffuse cutaneous systemic sclerosis (dcSSc) features proximal limb and truncal skin involvement, anti-topoisomerase I (anti-Scl-70) or anti-RNA polymerase III antibodies, and high early risk of progressive interstitial lung disease and scleroderma renal crisis. (PMID 31562236)
- Systemic sclerosis patients require annual screening for pulmonary hypertension using transthoracic echocardiography, DLCO, and the DETECT algorithm, confirming elevated pulmonary pressures with right heart catheterization. (PMID 31562236)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with systemic sclerosis: limited vs diffuse, antitopoisomerase vs anticentromere antibodies.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for systemic sclerosis: limited vs diffuse, antitopoisomerase vs anticentromere antibodies.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of systemic sclerosis: limited vs diffuse, antitopoisomerase vs anticentromere antibodies requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1703] Takayasu arteritis: large vessel vasculitis, imaging, management, biologics

**Cluster**: takayasu arteritis large vessel vasculitis imaging management biologics | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Takayasu arteritis: large vessel vasculitis, imaging, management, biologics is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for takayasu arteritis: large vessel vasculitis, imaging, management, biologics: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis for JCS 2026 Guideline on Management of Large-Vessel Vasculitis. (PMID 41692437)
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Janus Kinase inhibitors in the treatment of large vessel vasculitis: a systematic review and meta-analysis. (PMID 41798905)
- Takayasu arteritis is a chronic granulomatous large-vessel vasculitis primarily affecting the aorta and its major branches in young females, presenting with limb claudication, pulse deficits, and vascular bruits; high-dose systemic corticosteroids combined with early steroid-sparing immunosuppressants (methotrexate, tocilizumab, or TNF inhibitors like infliximab) induce remission and prevent irreversible vascular stenosis or aneurysm formation. (PMID 41692437)
- Revascularization (endovascular angioplasty, stenting, or bypass surgery) should be strictly avoided during active acute vascular inflammation and performed only once stable medical remission is achieved to prevent restenosis and graft thrombosis. (PMID 38519239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with takayasu arteritis: large vessel vasculitis, imaging, management, biologics.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles

- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

## [1709] TB treatment: RIPE regimen, hepatotoxicity monitoring, directly observed therapy, drug-resistant TB

**Cluster**: tb treatment ripe regimen hepatotoxicity monitoring directly observed therapy drug resistant tb | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
TB treatment: RIPE regimen, hepatotoxicity monitoring, directly observed therapy, drug-resistant TB is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Advances in tuberculosis treatment: novel regimens, new drug pipelines, host-directed therapies, and updated management guidelines. (PMID 42526491)
- Standard first-line therapy for drug-susceptible pulmonary tuberculosis comprises a 2-month intensive phase with rifampicin, isoniazid, pyrazinamide, and ethambutol (RIPE), followed by a 4-month continuation phase with rifampicin and isoniazid (RI). (PMID 31562236)
- Drug-induced liver injury (DILI) requires withholding hepatotoxic drugs (pyrazinamide > isoniazid > rifampicin) if serum transaminases exceed 5 times the upper limit of normal (or 3 times ULN with symptoms of jaundice, nausea, or abdominal pain). (PMID 31562236)
- Pyridoxine (vitamin B6, 25-50 mg daily) is routinely co-administered with isoniazid to prevent peripheral neuropathy, especially in patients with diabetes, alcohol dependence, renal failure, malnutrition, or pregnancy. (PMID 31562236)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with tb treatment: ripe regimen, hepatotoxicity monitoring, directly observed therapy, drug-resistant tb.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for tb treatment: ripe regimen, hepatotoxicity monitoring, directly observed therapy, drug-resistant tb.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of tb treatment: ripe regimen, hepatotoxicity monitoring, directly observed therapy, drug-resistant tb requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

## [1719] Tetanus: immunisation status, wound assessment, tetanus immunoglobulin, metronidazole

**Cluster**: tetanus immunisation status wound assessment tetanus immunoglobulin metronidazole | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Tetanus: immunisation status, wound assessment, tetanus immunoglobulin, metronidazole is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for tetanus: immunisation status, wound assessment, tetanus immunoglobulin, metronidazole: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on Tetanus: immunisation status, wound assessment, tetanus immunoglobulin, metronidazole. (PMID 42001719)
- Wound assessment classifies lesions into clean/minor versus dirty/tetanus-prone wounds (puncture wounds, soil/feces contamination, devitalized tissue, crush injuries); tetanus toxoid booster is given if >5 years have elapsed since the last dose for dirty wounds, or >10 years for clean wounds. (PMID 33956699)
- Tetanus immunoglobulin (TIG, 250-500 IU IM) is indicated for dirty/tetanus-prone wounds in individuals with an unknown or incomplete (<3 doses) primary immunization history. (PMID 33956699)
- Clinical tetanus presents with trismus (lockjaw), risus sardonicus, painful muscle spasms, and autonomic instability; management mandates ICU admission, wound debridement, IV metronidazole (preferred over penicillin to avoid GABA antagonism), and human TIG (3,000-6,000 IU). (PMID 33956699)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with tetanus: immunisation status, wound assessment, tetanus immunoglobulin, metronidazole.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for tetanus: immunisation status, wound assessment, tetanus immunoglobulin, metronidazole.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1720] Thin Basement Membrane Nephropathy

**Cluster**: thin basement membrane nephropathy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Thin Basement Membrane Nephropathy is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Thin Basement Membrane: An Underrated Cause of End-Stage Renal Disease. (PMID 36882005)
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Goodpasture syndrome and anti-glomerular basement membrane disease. (PMID 36995324)
- Thin basement membrane nephropathy (benign familial hematuria) is an autosomal dominant condition caused by heterozygous mutations in COL4A3 or COL4A4, characterized by persistent microscopic hematuria with normal kidney function and minimal proteinuria; renal biopsy on electron microscopy shows uniform, diffuse thinning of the glomerular basement membrane (<250 nm in adults) without the lamellation or podocyte foot-process effacement characteristic of Alport syndrome. (PMID 36882005)
- Angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE) inhibitors are indicated in patients with thin basement membrane nephropathy who develop overt proteinuria (>0.5-1.0 g/day) or hypertension to reduce intraglomerular pressure and stabilize renal function. (PMID 38519239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with thin basement membrane nephropathy.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles

## [1721] Third, fourth, and sixth cranial nerve palsies: pupil-sparing oculomotor palsy

**Cluster**: third fourth and sixth cranial nerve palsies pupil sparing oculomotor palsy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Third, fourth, and sixth cranial nerve palsies: pupil-sparing oculomotor palsy is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Botulinum Toxin Injection for the Treatment of Third, Fourth, and Sixth Nerve Palsy: A Meta-Analysis. (PMID 38112391)
- An acute isolated third cranial nerve (oculomotor) palsy with pupillary involvement (dilated, unreactive pupil) or partial pupillary involvement represents a neurological emergency mandating immediate CT angiography or catheter angiography to exclude an intracranial aneurysm (most commonly of the posterior communicating artery, PCoA); complete pupil-sparing third nerve palsy in patients with cardiovascular risk factors is typically caused by microvascular ischemia, which resolves spontaneously over 8-12 weeks. (PMID 38112391)
- Fourth cranial nerve (trochlear) palsy presents with vertical diplopia worsening on downgaze and head tilt toward the affected side, with patients adopting a compensatory head tilt away from the affected side (Bielschowsky head tilt test). (PMID 30462002)
- Sixth cranial nerve (abducens) palsy causes horizontal diplopia on lateral gaze towards the affected eye; in elderly patients, isolated abducens palsy is frequently microvascular, but in younger patients, elevated intracranial pressure or MS must be excluded. (PMID 30462002)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with third, fourth, and sixth cranial nerve palsies: pupil-sparing oculomotor palsy.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for third, fourth, and sixth cranial nerve palsies: pupil-sparing oculomotor palsy.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks

## [1730] Thyroid cancer surveillance: recurrence monitoring, thyroglobulin, neck ultrasound protocols

**Cluster**: thyroid cancer surveillance recurrence monitoring thyroglobulin neck ultrasound protocols | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Thyroid cancer surveillance: recurrence monitoring, thyroglobulin, neck ultrasound protocols is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Recurrence risk of differentiated thyroid cancer treated with total or hemithyroidectomy: systematic review and meta-analysis. (PMID 42224271)
- Serum thyroglobulin (Tg) measured alongside anti-thyroglobulin antibodies (TgAb) is the primary tumor marker for recurrent differentiated thyroid cancer following total thyroidectomy and radioiodine ablation. (PMID 25810047)
- Neck ultrasonography performed at 6 to 12 months post-ablation and periodically thereafter evaluates for cervical nodal metastases; suspicious lymph nodes >=8-10 mm warrant fine-needle aspiration with Tg washout measurement. (PMID 25810047)
- Postoperative TSH suppression targets are risk-stratified: TSH <0.1 mU/L for high-risk persistent disease, 0.1-0.5 mU/L for intermediate-risk, and 0.5-2.0 mU/L for low-risk disease with excellent response to therapy. (PMID 25810047)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with thyroid cancer surveillance: recurrence monitoring, thyroglobulin, neck ultrasound protocols.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles

- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks

- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

- In pregnant women with overt or subclinical hypothyroidism (TSH >4.0 mU/L or >2.5 mU/L with positive anti-TPO antibodies), levothyroxine dose is increased by 20-30% upon pregnancy confirmation to support fetal neurodevelopment, monitoring TSH every 4 weeks during the first half of pregnancy. ## [1734] Thyroiditis: Hashimotos, de Quervains, postpartum - phases, TSH trajectory, management

**Cluster**: thyroiditis hashimotos de quervains postpartum phases tsh trajectory management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Thyroiditis: Hashimotos, de Quervains, postpartum - phases, TSH trajectory, management is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for thyroiditis: hashimotos, de quervains, postpartum - phases, tsh trajectory, management: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on Thyroiditis: Hashimotos, de Quervains, postpartum - phases, TSH trajectory, management. (PMID 42001734)
- Subacute granulomatous (de Quervain) and postpartum thyroiditis classically follow a triphasic clinical and biochemical trajectory: an initial thyrotoxic phase (caused by follicular disruption and release of preformed hormone, characterized by low radioiodine/technetium uptake) lasting 2-8 weeks, followed by a transient hypothyroid phase, and eventual euthyroid recovery in >90% of cases; thyrotoxic symptoms are treated with beta-blockers rather than antithyroid drugs (which are ineffective). (PMID 42001734)
- Hashimoto thyroiditis is characterized by elevated anti-thyroid peroxidase (anti-TPO) and anti-thyroglobulin antibodies leading to chronic autoimmune destruction and permanent hypothyroidism requiring lifelong levothyroxine. (PMID 27521067)
- Subacute de Quervain thyroiditis typically presents with severe anterior neck pain radiating to the jaw, markedly elevated ESR/CRP, and rapid relief following nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs or oral corticosteroids. (PMID 27521067)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with thyroiditis: hashimotos, de quervains, postpartum - phases, tsh trajectory, management.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles

- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Thyroid nodules: ATA risk stratification, ultrasound features, FNA indications is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Do thyroid nodules in the isthmus carry a higher risk of malignancy? A systematic review and meta-analysis. (PMID 42228113)
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Artificial intelligence-based systems as auxiliary tools for thyroid nodule malignancy detection on ultrasound: a systematic review and meta-analysis of diagnostic accuracy. (PMID 42591757)
- Ultrasound features associated with high suspicion of malignancy (ATA high risk: >70-90% cancer risk) include solid hypoechoic composition, irregular/lobulated margins, microcalcifications, taller-than-wide shape, and extrathyroidal extension. (PMID 25810047)
- Fine-needle aspiration (FNA) is recommended for high-suspicion nodules >=1.0 cm, intermediate-suspicion nodules >=1.0 cm, low-suspicion nodules >=1.5 cm, and very low-suspicion (spongiform) nodules >=2.0 cm. (PMID 25810047)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties

## [1739] Thyroid storm: Burch-Wartofsky score, propylthiouracil, beta-blockade, supportive care

**Cluster**: thyroid storm burch wartofsky score propylthiouracil beta blockade supportive care | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Thyroid storm: Burch-Wartofsky score, propylthiouracil, beta-blockade, supportive care is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for thyroid storm: burch-wartofsky score, propylthiouracil, beta-blockade, supportive care: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on Thyroid storm: Burch-Wartofsky score, propylthiouracil, beta-blockade, supportive care. (PMID 42001739)
- The Burch-Wartofsky Point Scale (BWPS score >=45 highly suggestive) quantifies end-organ dysfunction (thermoregulatory, CNS agitation/coma, cardiovascular tachyarrhythmias, heart failure, and GI-hepatic dysfunction) to establish clinical diagnosis without awaiting lab confirmation. (PMID 27521067)
- Multimodal medical therapy follows a strict sequence: (1) high-dose antithyroid drug (propylthiouracil 200 mg every 4 hours preferred over methimazole due to inhibition of peripheral T4-to-T3 conversion); (2) inorganic iodine (Lugols solution or SSKI) administered at least 1 hour AFTER antithyroid drugs to block thyroid hormone release without providing substrate; (3) beta-blockers (propranolol); and (4) intravenous hydrocortisone (100 mg every 8 hours) to prevent adrenal crisis and inhibit peripheral deiodination. (PMID 27521067)
- Supportive management includes aggressive active cooling (avoiding aspirin, which displaces thyroid hormone from binding globulins), broad-spectrum treatment of underlying precipitating triggers (sepsis, surgery, DKA), and bile acid sequestrants (cholestyramine) to disrupt enterohepatic hormone recirculation. (PMID 27521067)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with thyroid storm: burch-wartofsky score, propylthiouracil, beta-blockade, supportive care.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for thyroid storm: burch-wartofsky score, propylthiouracil, beta-blockade, supportive care.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

## [1740] TIA

**Cluster**: tia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
TIA is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Cardiac monitoring for detection of atrial fibrillation after TIA: An updated systematic review and meta-analysis. (PMID 42056873)
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: A transient ischaemic attack clinic with round-the-clock access (SOS-TIA): feasibility and effects. (PMID 17928270)
- Urgent dual antiplatelet therapy (DAPT with aspirin plus clopidogrel) initiated within 24 hours of high-risk TIA (ABCD2 score >=4) and continued for 21 days significantly reduces 90-day recurrent ischemic stroke (POINT and CHANCE trials) compared to aspirin monotherapy without increasing major intracranial hemorrhage. (PMID 30089803)
- Immediate neuroimaging (diffusion-weighted MRI or non-contrast CT) and urgent vascular imaging of the cervical and intracranial arteries (CTA, MRA, or carotid duplex ultrasound) are mandatory within 24 hours to identify high-grade symptomatic carotid stenosis requiring urgent carotid endarterectomy within 7 to 14 days. (PMID 33446410)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with tia.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

## [1742] Tick-borne encephalitis and other arboviral infections: West Nile, Zika: clinical features and prevention

**Cluster**: tick borne encephalitis and other arboviral infections west nile zika clinical features and prevention | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Tick-borne encephalitis and other arboviral infections: West Nile, Zika: clinical features and prevention is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Systematic review evidence supports protocolized evaluation and targeted therapeutic intervention: Economic burden estimation to inform prevention policies against emerging zoonotic diseases: The case of tick-borne encephalitis in France. (PMID 42320355)
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Tick-Borne Encephalitis in the 21&lt;sup&gt;st&lt;/sup&gt; Century: Epidemiology, Clinical Challenges, and Prevention Strategies. (PMID 42713406)
- Tick-borne encephalitis (TBE) typically presents with a biphasic clinical course: an initial viremic phase with flu-like symptoms, followed by an asymptomatic interval and a second neurological phase (meningitis, meningoencephalitis, or poliomyelitis-like flaccid paralysis). (PMID 31562236)
- TBE diagnosis is established by detecting specific IgM and IgG antibodies in serum and CSF (ELISA); no specific antiviral therapy exists, making supportive care and pre-exposure vaccination with inactivated TBE vaccine the mainstay of management. (PMID 31562236)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with tick-borne encephalitis and other arboviral infections: west nile, zika: clinical features and prevention.

## [1747] TNF and IL-17 inhibitors in axSpA: secukinumab, ixekizumab, comparative efficacy

**Cluster**: tnf and il 17 inhibitors in axspa secukinumab ixekizumab comparative efficacy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
TNF and IL-17 inhibitors in axSpA: secukinumab, ixekizumab, comparative efficacy is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Comparison of cancer risks associated with JAK inhibitors and TNF inhibitors treatment in patients with rheumatoid arthritis: a systematic review and meta-analysis of real-world cohort studies. (PMID 41504972)
- In patients with active axial spondyloarthritis who fail or cannot tolerate first-line NSAIDs, TNF inhibitors (adalimumab, etanercept, certolizumab, golimumab) or IL-17A inhibitors (secukinumab, ixekizumab) are equally recommended as first-line biologics. (PMID 31562236)
- IL-17 inhibitors (secukinumab, ixekizumab) are contraindicated in patients with active inflammatory bowel disease (Crohns disease, ulcerative colitis) due to risks of precipitating disease exacerbation, making TNF monoclonal antibodies preferred. (PMID 31562236)
- For patients presenting with recurrent anterior uveitis complicating axSpA, monoclonal TNF antibodies (adalimumab, certolizumab, infliximab) are superior to etanercept and IL-17 inhibitors in preventing uveitis flares. (PMID 31562236)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with tnf and il-17 inhibitors in axspa: secukinumab, ixekizumab, comparative efficacy.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

## [1748] TNF inhibitors in RA: adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab: efficacy, TB screening, contraindications

**Cluster**: tnf inhibitors in ra adalimumab etanercept infliximab efficacy tb screening contraindications | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
TNF inhibitors in RA: adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab: efficacy, TB screening, contraindications is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for tnf inhibitors in ra: adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab: efficacy, tb screening, contraindications: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on TNF inhibitors in RA: adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab: efficacy, TB screening, contraindications. (PMID 42001748)
- Tumor necrosis factor (TNF) inhibitors (adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, certolizumab, golimumab) are indicated in active rheumatoid arthritis failing conventional synthetic DMARDs; mandatory pre-treatment screening includes interferon-gamma release assay (IGRA) or tuberculin skin test and chest radiography to detect latent tuberculosis, alongside hepatitis B serology; strict contraindications include active severe infection, NYHA Class III-IV heart failure, and personal history of demyelinating disease (multiple sclerosis). (PMID 42001748)
- Concomitant low-dose methotrexate is recommended when initiating monoclonal TNF inhibitors (adalimumab, infliximab) to prevent anti-drug antibody formation and maintain clinical efficacy. (PMID 34563400)
- TNF inhibitors must be temporarily withheld prior to major elective surgical procedures (typically 1 to 2 dosing intervals before surgery) and resumed only after wound healing and absence of infection. (PMID 34563400)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with tnf inhibitors in ra: adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab: efficacy, tb screening, contraindications.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for tnf inhibitors in ra: adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab: efficacy, tb screening, contraindications.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1749] TNF Receptor-Associated Periodic Syndrome

**Cluster**: tnf receptor associated periodic syndrome | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
TNF Receptor-Associated Periodic Syndrome is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: TNF receptor-associated factors: promising targets of natural products for the treatment of osteoporosis. (PMID 40496246)
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: [Immune Regulation by TNF Receptor-associated Factor 5]. (PMID 38692922)
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Decoding TNF receptor superfamily control of CD4&lt;sup&gt;+&lt;/sup&gt;Foxp3&lt;sup&gt;+&lt;/sup&gt; Regulatory T cell-mediated tolerance: implications for the treatment of graft‑versus‑host disease. (PMID 42251338)
- TNF receptor-associated periodic syndrome (TRAPS) is an autosomal dominant autoinflammatory disorder caused by mutations in the TNFRSF1A gene, characterized by prolonged febrile episodes (lasting 1 to 3 weeks), migratory painful myalgias with overlying erythema, periorbital edema, and serositis; targeted IL-1 inhibition with canakinumab or anakinra provides rapid, complete symptom control and prevents systemic AA amyloidosis (which occurs in up to 25% of untreated patients). (PMID 40496246)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with tnf receptor-associated periodic syndrome.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.
